## Page 1


APPLICATIONS OF (HIGHER) CATEGORICAL TRACE II:
DELIGNE-LUSZTIG THEORY
D. GAITSGORY, N. ROZENBLYUM AND Y. VARSHAVSKY
To George Lusztig
Abstract. We use the formalism of the (2-category) AGCat, developed in [GRV], and the opera-
tion of higher categorical trace to (re)derive a number of results in the Deligne-Lusztig theory.

APPLICATIONS OF (HIGHER) CATEGORICAL TRACE II: DELIGNE-LUSZTIG THEORY
3
Introduction
0.1. What is this paper about? Let G be a connected reductive group over the ground field k. For
the applications in this paper we take k = Fq, the algebraic closure of a finite field Fq.
0.1.1.
In [GRV] we introduced a (symmetric monoidal) (∞, 2)-category AGCat, which is a version of
the (usual) (∞, 2)-category DGCat of DG categories (with coefficients Qℓ) that is better compatible
with the theory of ℓ-adic sheaves.
Namely, the assignment
(X ∈Sch) 7→(Shv(X) ∈AGCat)
is strictly symmetric monoidal, i.e., the functor
(0.1)
Shv(X1) ⊗Shv(X2) →Shv(X1 × X2)
is an equivalence (by design). This is in contrast with
(X ∈Sch) 7→(Shv(X) ∈DGCat),
for which the functor
Shv(X1) ⊗Shv(X2) →Shv(X1 × X2)
is only fully faithful (and it never an equivalence unless one of the schemes is a union of points).
0.1.2.
The category AGCat is a formal device (its definition has no algebro-geometric or categorical
content). Yet, it is useful.
In this paper, we will apply AGCat to develop a theory of categorical representations of G and to
give conceptual proofs of a number of results about character sheaves and Deligne-Lusztig functors.
We establish the independence of the Deligne-Lusztig functor of the parabolic at the level characters
(which seems to have been known in full generality).
0.1.3.
Here is how AGCat is used in this paper:
Given an algebraic group G, we define a 2-category G - mod of categorical representations of G,
where by a categorical representation we will mean one on an object of AGCat (rather than a more
naive notion, on which the action would be on an object of DGCat).
A typical example of an object of G - mod is Shv(Y), where Y is a scheme (or, more generally, an
arbitrary prestack) acted on by G.
0.2. Categorical representations: general theory.
0.2.1.
The fact that (0.1) is an equivalence implies that G - mod is a very well-behaved object. Here
are the three main tools that we will use to develop the theory:
• The fact that for an endomorphism ϕ of G, the (2-categorical) trace Tr(ϕ, G - mod) ∈AGCat
identifies with Shv(G/ Adϕ(G)), where Adϕ(G) refers the action of G on itself by ϕ-twisted
conjugacy. The two main cases of interest are when ϕ = id and ϕ = Frob;
• The induction functor
indP : M - mod →G - mod,
where G is reductive, P ⊂G is a parabolic, and M is the Levi quotient of P;
• For a pair (P1, P2) of associated parabolics, a natural transformation
(0.2)
indP1
IP1,P2
→
indP2,
which we call the intertwining functor.
These three pieces of structure combine to produce some interesting, albeit well-known, constructions
in geometric representation theory.

and we show that it equals the tautological identification.

0.2.5.
For a Levi subgroup M defined over Fq and a parabolic
M ,→P ↠M
(but P is not necessarily defined over Fq), the natural transformation IP,Frob(P ) gives rise to a natural
transformation
indP ◦FrobM →FrobG ◦indP ,
and hence to a functor
Tr(Frob, indP ) : Tr(FrobM, M - mod) →Tr(FrobG, G - mod).
Thus we obtain a functor
(0.8)
Rep(M(Fq)) ≃Tr(FrobM, M - mod)

−→
Tr(FrobG, G - mod) ≃Rep(G(Fq)).
We identify (0.8) with the functor, induced by the Deligne-Lusztig functor introduced in [DL, Lu1]
(see also [DM])
DLP : Rep(M(Fq)) →Rep(G(Fq)).
0.2.6.
However, this is as far as we could take the theory with the above tools.
0.3. Categorical representations:
the spectrally finite subcategory. Now, the theory gets
richer once we replace the entire G - mod by its full subcategory that we denote by
G - modspec.fin ⊂G - mod;
we refer the reader to Sect. 0.6 for the definition and to Sect. 5.1 for complete details.
0.3.1.
We show that the (full) subcategory
Tr(id, G - modspec.fin) ⊂Tr(id, G - mod)
identifies with the subcategory of character sheaves
Shvch(G/ AdFrob(G)) ⊂Shv(G/ AdFrob(G)).
This essentially a paraphrase of a result of D. Ben-Zvi and D. Nadler.
0.3.2.
We also show that the (full) subcategory
Tr(Frob, G - modspec.fin) ⊂Tr(Frob, G - mod)
is entire category Rep(G(Fq)); this a paraphrase of a result of A. Eteve.
0.3.3.
We use this to show that the “double trace” isomorphism (0.7) restricts to an isomorphism
Funct(G(Fq)/ Ad(G(Fq)), Qℓ) ≃Tr(id, Rep(G(Fq)) ≃Tr(id, Tr(Frob, G - modspec.fin)) ≃
≃Tr(Frob, Tr(id, G - modspec.fin)),
leading to the conclusion that
(0.9)
Tr(Frob, Shvch(G/ Ad(G))) ≃Funct((G/ Ad(G))(Fq), Qℓ).
Note that in the above formula, we are considering the object Shvch(G/ Ad(G)) ∈DGCat rather
than Shvch(G/ Ad(G)) ∈AGCat. One can view (0.9) as a counterpart for character sheaves of the
Trace Conjecture from [AGKRRV1].
Remark 0.3.4. The passage
Shvch(G/ Ad(G)) ⇝Shvch(G/ Ad(G))
above is part of the general phenomenon: as we shall see in Theorem 5.1.10, the passage
G - mod ⇝G - modspec.fin
gets rid of the extra “algebro-geometric” directions inherent to objects of AGCat, see Sect. 0.6.6.

We will refer to (0.11) as the functional equation for the Grothendieck-Springer functors. We perceive
it as an analog of the functional equation for geometric Eisenstein series in the geometric Langlands
theory, see [BG].
Remark 0.3.6. As was shown by S. Gunningham, an analog of the functional equation (0.11) would be
false if we did not restrict ourselves to character sheaves.
0.3.7.
A naive attempt to extend the functional equation to the Deligne-Lustig functor fails (for some
obvious reasons, see Remark 3.4.11). And indeed, it cannot hold, since it is known that DLP , viewed
as a functor, does depend on the choice of P.
However, it was conjectured in [Lu1] and established in many cases in [DL, Lu2, BM] (see a summary
in [Lu3, 0.1] or [DM, Chapter 9]) that the map
(0.12)
ch(DLP ) : Funct((M/ Ad(M))(Fq)) →Funct((G/ Ad(G))(Fq), Qℓ)
is independent of the choice of P.
This independence is one of the two main new results of this paper; it appears as Theorem 8.7.2.
We outline the proof in the next subsection.

Remark 0.4.2. The equality of (0.12) and (0.13) for q ≫0 is the main result of [Lu3]; it is also
conjectured in loc.cit. that the assumption that q ≫0 can be removed.
A particular case of the equality of (0.12) and (0.13) is the following statement established in [Lu2]:
Let M = T be a torus, and let χ be a 1-dimensional character sheaf on T equipped with a Weil
structure. On the one hand, we can form the Grothendieck-Springer sheaf GrothSprB(χ). We equip it
with a Weil structure, by first defining on the regular semi-simple locus of G (using the W-action and
the Weil structure on χ), and then applying the Goresky-MacPherson extension.
On the one hand, we consider the function
funct(GrothSprB(χ)) ∈Funct((G/ Ad(G))(Fq)),
obtained by taking pointwise traces of Frobenius on GrothSprB(χ) with respect to the above Weil
structure.
On the other hand, we consider the function
ch(DLB(χ0)) ∈Funct(G(Fq)/ Ad(G(Fq)), Qℓ).
where χ0 is the character of T(Fq), obtained by taking pointwise traces of Frobenius on χ (i.e., χ0 =
funct(χ)).
Then the equality of (0.12) and (0.13) in particular implies that
(0.14)
funct((GrothSprB(χ)) = ch(DLB(χ0))
as elements of
Funct((G/ Ad(G))(Fq), Qℓ) = Funct(G(Fq)/ Ad(G(Fq)), Qℓ).
Note that the usual proof of (0.14) (see, e.g., [Laum, Theorem 2.3.1], which follows [Lu2]) uses
some non-trivial computations involving Jordan decomposition. By contrast, our proof is a diagram
chase (once we accept the formula for local terms in the Grothendieck-Lefschetz fixed point theorem,
as generalized in [GaVa]).
0.4.3.

map (0.13) is.

equation (0.11), the functors GrothSprch

0.4.4.
First, there is the easy case, namely, when the relative positions of (P1, P2) and (P2, P3) add
up. In this case, we have
(0.16)

as natural transformations

and (0.15) is obtained by taking Tr(Id, −) on (0.16).
0.4.5.
Thus, by induction on the distance between P1 and P2, we reduce the verification of (0.15) to
the case when P3 = P1. I.e., we need to show that
(0.17)
iP2,P1 ◦iP1,P2 ≃idGrothSprch
P1 .
Note that it is not at all true that
(0.18)
IP2,P1 ◦IP1,P2
is the identity endomorphism of indP1.
In fact, this endomorphism is closely related to the Serre
functor, see Sect. 0.5.5.
Yet, it so happens that the non-triviality of (0.18) gets swallowed by the operation Tr(Id, −). We
prove this in Theorem 7.1.6.
0.4.6.
We find it curious that manipulations involving a 3-category (in our case 2 -AGCat) can be
used to prove a non-trivial equality of (ℓ-adic) numbers (which is what the independence of (0.12) of
the parabolic is). The proof essentially boils down to checking commutativity of huge diagrams, but
after packaging them so as to make it humanly possible.
At its most complex, one needs to show that two certain 3-morphisms (in a 3-category) are equal.
These 3-morphisms arise by taking units and counits of adjunctions of some 1-morphisms (these are
2-morphisms), then taking their adjoints, and considering 3-morphisms that are units and counits of
these adjunctions. An example of this is Corollary 1.8.7, which stands behind the equality of (0.12)
and (0.13).
A related strategy, but one that occurs at one categorical level lower (i.e., one works with the 2-
category DGCat rather than with the 3-category 2 -AGCat) was realized in [KP] for the proof of the
Riemann-Roch theorem.
0.5. What else is done in this paper? We will now explain how the 3-categorical perspective allows
one to reprove a number of results about character sheaves.
0.5.1.
Consider again the functor
indP : M - mod →G - mod.
It admits a right adjoint, called the Jacquet functor
M - mod ←G - mod : jacqP .
Moreover, this adjunction has the following property: the unit and counit natural transformations
(0.19)
unit : IdM - mod →jacqP ◦indP and counit : indP ◦jacqP →IdG - mod
themselves admit right adjoints.
Passing to these right adjoints, we obtain the natural transformations
unitR : jacqP ◦indP →IdM - mod and counitR : IdG - mod →indP ◦jacqP ,
which make jacqP into a left adjoint of indP .
Thus, we obtain that the (indP , jacqP )-adjunction is ambidexterous.

0.6. Monodromicity and restricted categorical representations. As was mentioned above, the
more interesting results of this paper require that we work with the subcategory
G - modspec.fin ⊂G - mod.
We will now explain its definition.
0.6.1.
Let us first take G = T to be the torus.
We shall say that an an object C ∈T - mod is
monodromic if it is generated by its (T, χ)-invariants, where χ runs over all possible character sheaves
on T.
We let
T - modspec.fin ⊂T - mod
consist of monodromic objects.
0.6.2.
For a general reductive G, we let G - modspec.fin to be the full subcategory consisting of objects
for which
invN(C) ∈T - mod
is monodromic. In the above formula, invN is the functor of invariants with respect to N ⊂B.
0.6.3. Example. Consder
Shv(G/B) ∈G - mod.
This object belongs to G - modspec.fin.
Consider now
Shv(G/N) ∈G - mod.
This object does not belong to G - modspec.fin.
0.6.4.
Of course, one needs to prove something in order to make the above definition useful. The key
fact is that the functor
invN : G - mod →AGCat,
which naturally upgrades to a functor
(0.24)
invenh
N
: G - mod →H - mod(AGCat),
H := Shv(N\G/N),
with the latter functor being an equivalence, according to a theorem of [BGO] (reproved in the present
paper as Theorem 5.2.4).
Thus, the knowledge of invN(C) (equipped with an action of H) fully recovers C.

0.6.5.
Let
Hmon ⊂H
be the full subcategory that consists of T-monodromic objects (with respect to the action of T on
N\G/N from the left/right, or equivalently, both sides). The above embedding admits a right adjoint.
Moreover, the object Hmon ∈AGCat carries a unique structure associative algebra for which the above
right adjoint
H →Hmon
is a homomorphism.
We can identify
G - modspec.fin ≃Hmon - mod(AGCat).
0.6.6.
One of the key points of the spec.fin theory is that it effectively takes place in the usual world
of DG categories, rather than AGCat:
Let
i : DGCat →AGCat
be the tautological embedding (see [GRV, Sect. 2.4.1]). The point is that Hmon (unlike H!) lies in the
essential image of this functor, i.e.,
Hmon = i(Hmon),
Hmon := Shv(N\G/N)mon.
Set
G - modrestr := Hmon-mod(DGCat) ∈2 -DGCat .
We obtain that
(0.25)
G - modspec.fin ≃AGCat
⊗
DGCatG - modrestr.
I.e., G - modspec.fin is the extension of scalars of a 2-category that is a module over the usual DGCat.
This implies among other things that
(0.26)
Shvch(G/ Ad(G)) ≃i(Shvch(G/ Ad(G))).
0.6.7.
The equivalence (0.25) means that the entire restricted/spectrally finite theory can be rewritten
without ever mentioning AGCat: one can just work with the 2-category
Hmon-mod(DGCat),
and one can transcribe all the proofs in its terms.
The advantage of using AGCat is that it gives a clearer geometric picture, which suggests natural
proofs of many results.
A vague analogy is: to think about representations of a p-adic group as such vs as a collection of
modules for Hecke algebras.
0.7. The hidden motivation for writing this paper. One of the key motivations for writing this
paper is that it is supposed to be a prelude to the case when instead of the usual reductive group G,
one considers its loop group L(G).
0.7.1.
One can define the object
L(G) - mod ∈2 -DGCat
in a way completely parallel to G - mod.
We have a well-defined object
Shv(L(G)/ Ad(L(G)) ∈AGCat
and an identification
Tr(Id, L(G) - mod) ≃Shv(L(G)/ Ad(L(G)).


## Page 12


12
D. GAITSGORY, N. ROZENBLYUM AND Y. VARSHAVSKY
0.7.2.
Note, however, that Lang’s theorem fails for L(G). The quotient
L(F)/ AdFrob(L(G)) =: IsocG
is the stack of isocrystals, extensively studied recently by X. Zhu in [Zhu]. One can think of IsocG as
an algebro-geometric incarnation of the Fargues-Scholze BunG(FFCurve).
As in (0.6), we have
Tr(Frob, L(G) - mod) ≃Shv(L(F)/ AdFrob(L(G))) = Shv(IsocG).
0.7.3.
Here are some basic questions that one wants to ask in the loop case:
(1) Is it true that the loop analog of (0.5), i.e., the functor
i(Shv(IsocG)) →Shv(IsocG),
is an equivalence?
(2) What is the subcategory L(G) - modspec.fin ⊂L(G) - mod, so that
L(G) - modspec.fin ≃AGCat
⊗
DGCatL(G) - modrestr
for L(G) - modrestr ∈2 -DGCat?
(3) Is it true that an analog of Eteve’s theorem holds, i.e., the (fully faithful) functor
Tr(Frob, L(G) - modspec.fin) →Tr(Frob, L(G) - mod) ≃Shv(IsocG)
is an equivalence?
(4) What is the definition of
Shvch(L(G)/ Ad(L(G)) ⊂Shv(L(G)/ Ad(L(G))?
(5) Is it true that
Tr(Id, L(G) - modspec.fin) = Shvch(L(G)/ Ad(L(G))
as subcategories of
Tr(Id, L(G) - mod) ≃Shv(L(G)/ Ad(L(G)),
respectively.
0.7.4.
Here are some tentative answers:
First, it seems that Question (1) can be settled affirmatively using the results of [Zhu].
Question (2) is far more complicated than in the finite-dimensional case, because we do not have
anything resembling the equivalence of (0.24).
Rather, a promising approach seems to characterize L(G) - modspec.fin using a (geometric analog)
of the Fintzen-Yu classification (or its refinement, recently found by [DVY]).
As to Question (3), it does seem plausible that the method of proof of Eteve’s theorem (see Theo-
rem 8.1.3) is applicable and gives the desired result.
Finally, for Questions (4) and (5) the best we can do is cheat and define
Shvch(L(G)/ Ad(L(G)) := Tr(Id, L(G) - modspec.fin).
0.8. Structure of the paper. We now briefly describe the contents of this paper section-by-section.


## Page 13


APPLICATIONS OF (HIGHER) CATEGORICAL TRACE II: DELIGNE-LUSZTIG THEORY
13
0.8.1.
In Sect. 1 we explain how the formalism of trace plays out in the 2- and 3-categorical settings:
In the case of 1-categories, given a symmetric monoidal category O, a dualizable object o ∈O and
an endomorphism F of O, one attaches to it an endomorphism Tr(F, o) of the unit object 1O.
This gets more interesting for 2-categories: given two pairs (o1, F1) and (o2, F2) as above and a
1-morphism t : o1 →o2 that weakly intertwines F1 and F2, i.e, we have a natural transformation
t ◦F1
α→F2 ◦t,
we obtain a map
Tr(α, t) : Tr(F1, o1) →Tr(F2, o2),
provided that t is adjointable1, a notion that makes sense for a 1-morphism in a 2-category.
And this gets even richer (and more mind-twisting) for 3-categories.
This section summarizes the results about trace manipulations that we will apply in later sections
in representation-theoretic situations.
0.8.2.
In Sect. 2 we introduce the notion of categorical representation of an algebraic group. These
will form an object, denoted G - mod in the ambient 3-category that we denote 2 -AGCat (morally,
2-categories, tensored over AGCat).
We study basic concepts such as dualizability and adjointability of 1-morphisms.
One of the observations that will play a key role in this paper is that the 1-morphism
indP : M - mod →G - mod
is 2-adjointable, see Sect. 0.5.1.
0.8.3.
In Sect. 3 we perform the basic trace calculations in G - mod and for 1-morphisms
G1 - mod →G2 - mod.
In particular, we prove the calculations described in Sect. 0.2.
0.8.4.
In Sect. 4 we introduce the notion of monodromic action an algebraic group on an object of
AGCat. The actual case of interest is when the group in question is a torus, but we do this for any
algebraic group, just because this is a natural generality.
We start by introducing the subcategory
Shv(G)mon ⊂Shv(G),
which is the subcategory generated by 1-dimensional character sheaves. Having this definition, one can
define the monodromic subcategory
Shv(Y)mon ⊂Shv(Y)
for any prestack Y acted on by G. This, in turn, leads to the definition of Shv(G)mon which is a Hopf
algebra object in AGCat. That said, the object Shv(G)mon has the property that the canonical map
i(Shv(G)mon) →Shv(G)mon
is an isomorphism.
By definition,
(0.27)
G - modmon ⊂G - mod
is a full subcategory, consisting of objects, on which the co-action of Shv(G) factors through Shv(G)mon.
We show that the embedding (0.27) has very favorable properties (for example, it is 2-adjointable).
We also introduce a related notion:
Shv(G)q-mon ⊂Shv(G),
1In this paper “adjointable” means “admits a right adjoint”.


## Page 14


14
D. GAITSGORY, N. ROZENBLYUM AND Y. VARSHAVSKY
and its descendants
Shv(Y)q-mon ⊂Shv(Y), Shv(G)q-mon ⊂Shv(G), G - modq-mon ⊂G - mod
(when G is a torus, there is no difference between the two).
The reason for discussing the quasi-monodromic versions is the following. They provide an answer
to the following question: do there exists objects in G - mod, whose underlying object of AGCat lies
in the essential image of i : DGCat →AGCat? The answer is that such objects exactly correspond to
quasi-monodromic actions.
0.8.5.
In Sect. 5, we introduce the subcategories
G - modrestr ⊂G - modspec.fin ⊂G - mod
and establish the properties described in Sects. 0.3 and 0.6 above.
0.8.6.
In Sect. 6 we review the theory of character sheaves, and then recast from the perspective of
G - mod.
0.8.7.
In Sect. 7 we prove the transitivity property of the functional equation, mentioned in Sect. 0.4.3.
0.8.8.
Finally, in Sect. 8 we assemble all the ingredients and prove the independence of Deligne-Lusztig
characters of the parabolic, as outlined in Sect. 0.4.
0.8.9.
In Sect. A we supply the proofs for some technical statements from Sect. 4.
0.9. Conventions and notation.
0.9.1.
Our conventions and notations follow those adopted in [GRV].
0.9.2. Category theory. We will be working in the context of higher category theory. When we say
“category” we always mean an ∞-category.
We let Spc denote the category of spaces, a.k.a. groupoids.
Given a category C, and c1, c2 ∈C, we let
MapsC(c1, c2) ∈Spc
denote the corresponding space of morphisms.
For a category C, we let Cgrpd denote the space of its objects (i.e., the groupoid obtained from C
by discarding non-invertible morphisms).
0.9.3.
We will work also with (∞, 2) and (∞, 3)-categories. We adopt the inductive definition:
Having defined the (symmetric) monoidal (∞, 1)-category n -Cat of (∞, n)-categories, we define the
(symmetric) monoidal (∞, 1)-category (n + 1) -Cat to consist of (∞, 1)-categories enriched over n -Cat.
One then shows that n -Cat itself upgrades to an object of (n + 1) -Cat.
Given C ∈n -Cat and n′ ≤n, we let
Cn′ -Cat ∈n′ -Cat
be the object obtained by discarding non-invertible n′′-morphisms for n′ < n′′ ≤n.
Following the same conventions as in Sect. 0.9.2, in most cases, we will simply say “n-category”
when we mean an (∞, n)-category.

0.9.5. An identity crisis. When writing this paper we faced a certain dilemma regarding the usage of
symbols Id vs id. To the extent possible, we have tried to adhere to the following conventions:
For an object c in an (∞, 1)-category C, we denote by idc ∈MapsC(c, c) its identity endomorphism.
And we denote by IdC the identity endofunctor of C.
More generally, for a (∞, 2)-category O, and an object C ∈O we denote by IdC the identity 1-
morphism from C to itself.
For a 1-morphism ϕ : C1 →C2 in O, we let idϕ denote the identity
2-endomorphism of ϕ.
Now, this runs into a clash when we are dealing with an ambient (∞, 3)-category E. For a pair of ob-
jects O1, O2 ∈E and a 1-morphism Φ : O1 →O2 we will sometimes denote the identity endomorphism
of Φ by idΦ and sometimes by IdΦ, depending on the context:
We will use the former, when we want to de-emphasize the 3-categorical aspect of E (i.e., when for
the local discussion, non-invertible 3-morphisms do not play a big role). And we will use the latter
when O1 and O2 are fixed, and our focus is the (∞, 2)-category MapsE(O1, O2), in which Φ is an object.
0.9.6. Higher algebra. We will do higher algebra in vector spaces over the field of coefficients, which in
this paper we take to be Qℓ.
We let DGCat denote the (∞, 2)-category of Qℓ-linear DG categories, defined as in [GR, Chapter
1, Sect. 10].
We view DGCat as equipped with the symmetric monoidal structure, given by the Lurie tensor
product. The unit object for this structure is Vect, the category of (chain complexes) Qℓ-vector spaces.
For a DG category C and c1, c2 ∈C, we let
HomC(c1, c2) ∈Vect
denote the corresponding object. We have
MapsC(c1, c2) = τ ≤0(HomC(c1, c2)),
where we view the right-hand side as a connective spectrum.
0.9.7. Algebraic geometry. Throughout this paper we will be working with the category of (classical,
separated) schemes of finite type over a ground field k, assumed algebraically closed. We denote this
category by Sch. We will not need derived algebraic geometry over k for this paper.
Whenever we talk about the Frobenius endomorphism, we will assume that k = Fq, but the scheme
X in question is defined over Fq, so that the geometric Frobenius FrobX on X is defined.
By a prestack we shall mean a (classical) prestack locally of finite type over k (see [GR, Chapter 2,
Sect. 1.3.6] for what this means). We denote the corresponding category by PreStk.

We will work with the sheaf theory of ℓ-adic sheaves

as defined in [AGKRRV1, Sect. 1.1].
The above assignment extends to prestacks by the procedure of right Kan extension.
0.9.9. Groups. We let G be a linear algebraic group over k. For most of the paper, we will assume that

1. Higher categorical trace
The usual operation of trace takes a (dualizable!) object o in a symmetric monoidal category O, and
attaches to an endomorphism F of O an endomorphism Tr(F, o) of 1O. Now, when O is a symmetric
monoidal 2-category O, the trace operation interacts in a non-trivial way with 1-morphisms in O. And
this interaction becomes significantly richer when C is a 3-category.
We will describe these interactions in the present section.
1.1. Trace in 1-categories.
1.1.1.
Let O be a symmetric monoidal category. Let L(O) denote the (symmetric monoidal) groupoid
consisting of pairs (O, F), where o is a dualizable object of O and F is its endomorphism.
The trace construction is a (symmetric monoidal) functor
(1.1)
Tr : L(O) →End(1O) =: Ω(O).
1.1.2.
Explicitly, (1.1) sends F ∈End(o) to the composition

1.3. The notion of 2-dualizability.
1.3.1.
Let O be a symmetric monoidal 2-category.
Recall that an object o ∈O is said to be 2-
dualizable if the following conditions hold:
• o is dualizable as an object of O1 -Cat;
• The unit and counit 1-morphisms
uo : 1O →o ⊗o∨and evo : o ⊗o∨→1O
are adjointable.
1.3.2. Example. Let O = DGCat, so that o is a DG category C. Assume that C is compactly generated
(in particular, it is dualizable as an object of DGCat1 -Cat).
Then C is 2-dualizable if and only if it is smooth and proper.
Remark 1.3.3. Passing to the right adjoints of the unit and counit of the duality between o and o∨, we
obtain a different datum of duality. The discrepancy between the two is an automorphism of o, called
Serre functor, and denoted Seo.
2Here and in the sequel, “BC” stands for Beck-Chevalley.

2. Categorical representations
In this section we define the main object of study of this paper–the notion of categorical represen-
tation of a group.

and notations from [GRV, Sect. A.2].

For a monoidal (∞, 2)-category O and A ∈AssocAlg(O) we will denote by
A - mod(O)
the (∞, 2)-category of A-modules in O. I.e., we use boldface (- mod vs -mod) to emphasize the level
of categoricity.

(C vs C) for the former.

2.2. The notion of categorical representation.
2.2.1.
Recall that to any X ∈Sch we assign an object
(2.5)
Shv(X) ∈AGCat .
Since any X naturally upgrades to a cocommutative coalgebra object in Sch, the object Shv(X) is
naturally a cocommutative coalgebra in AGCat.
Remark 2.2.2. Note that the latter uses the fact that the functor
Shv(−) : Sch →AGCat
is strictly symmetric monoidal. This is the key difference between Shv(−) and the functor
Shv : Sch →DGCat,
which is only right-lax symmetric monoidal.

2.2.3.
Let now G be an algebraic group, i.e., a group-object in Sch. We obtain that Shv(G) carries a
structure of cocommutative Hopf algebra in AGCat.
2.2.4.
Recall also that for every X ∈Sch, the object (2.5) is canonically self-dual.
In particular, by duality, Shv(G) obtains a structure of commutative Hopf algebra. In order to avoid
confusion, we will notationally differentiate the two as follows:
• We will keep the symbol Shv(G) for the (commutative) Hopf algebra structure, in which the
comultiplication is induced by the functor mult!;
• We will use the symbol Shv(G)co for the (cocommutative) Hopf algebra structure, in which
the multiplication is induced by the functor mult∗.
Above and in the rest of the paper,
mult : G × G →G
is the group structure on G.
Note that the functors mult! and mult∗are each others’ duals, as they should be.
2.2.5.
Set
G - mod := Shv(G)co - mod(AGCat) ∈2 -AGCat .
Equivalently,
G - mod ≃Shv(G) - comod(AGCat).
For C ∈G - mod, we will denote by
act : Shv(G)co ⊗C →C
the corresponding 1-morphism that encodes the module structure.
2.2.6. Example. Let Y be a prestack equipped with an action of G. Then pullback with respect to the
action map G × Y →Y makes Shv(Y) an object of G - mod, thought of as Shv(G) - comod(AGCat).
The action of Shv(G)co is given by pushforward along the (schematic) map G × Y →Y.
Let f : Y1 →Y2 be a map between prestacks acted on by G. The functor
f ! : Shv(Y2) →Shv(Y1)
naturally upgrades to a 1-morphism in G - mod.

3.4.7]).
In this case we have the

f▲: Shv(Y1) →Shv(Y1)
(see [GRV, Sect. 3.7.3]), which also naturally upgrades to a 1-morphism in G - mod.

2.4. Dualilzability for categorical representations. Recall that our ability to perform the trace
construction necessitated the dualizability assumption on objects.
In this subsection we will see what this condition means for G - mod (at the 3-categorical and
2-categorical levels).
2.4.1.
First, we claim that
G - mod ∈2 -AGCat
is dualizable and self-dual:
(2.17)
(G - mod)∨≃G - mod.
Indeed, this is the case for objects of the form
A-mod(O) ∈O - mod,
where:
• O is a symmetric monoidal category, so O - mod is a symmetric monoidal 2-category;
• A is an associative algebra on O, equipped with an anti-involution.6
In our case O := AGCat and A := Shv(G)co, with the anti-involution provided by the antipode.
2.4.2.
Let H be a cocommutative Hopf algebra in a symmetric monoidal category O, so that
H-mod(O) acquires a symmetric monoidal structure compatible with the forgetful functor
oblvH : H-mod(O) →O.
Note that in this situation, an object of H-mod(O) is dualizable if and only if the underlying object
o ∈O is dualizable, and the dual is given by o∨with the natural right action, which we turn into a
left action using the antipode on H.
2.4.3.
In particular, we obtain that an object of G - mod is dualizable if and only if the underlying
object C ∈AGCat is dualizable, and the dual is given by C∨with the natural action.
In particular, we obtain that if Y is an AG tame prestack (see [GRV, Sect. 3.4.7]) equipped with an
action of G, then the object
Shv(Y) ∈G - mod
is dualizable and self-dual (by [GRV, Theorem 3.6.2 and Sect. 3.5.11]).
In particular, this is the case whenever Y is a pseudo-scheme (in particular, an ind-scheme) (see
[GRV, Lemma 3.4.5]).
Crucially for us, this is the case when Y is an algebraic stack that is locally a quotient of a scheme
by an algebraic group: this follows from [GRV, Proposition 4.6.4] and [AGKRRV1, Theorem F.2.8].
In fact, according to [AGKRRV1, Conjecture F.2.7], we expect this to be the case for any quasi-
compact algebraic stack with a schematic diagonal.
6A priori, the dual is Arev-mod, but the anti-involution provides an identification A ≃Arev (here and elsewhere,

2.6. Patterns of 2-adjointability for categorical representations.
2.6.1.
Let us now be given a pair of groups G1, G2 and a prestack Y acted on by G1 × G2. Consider

## Page 44


44
D. GAITSGORY, N. ROZENBLYUM AND Y. VARSHAVSKY
2.6.2.
We will say that a map
f : Y1 →Y2
between AG tame prestacks is mock-proper, if the corresponding 1-morphism
f▲: Shv(Y1) →Shv(Y2)
is adjointable (in AGCat).
We will say that an AG tame prestack Y is mock-proper if its map to pt is mock-proper.
It is easy to see that if f is mock-proper, then so is the map
f × id : Y1 × Z →Y2 × Z
for any AG tame prestack Z (recall that the class of AG tame prestacks is closed under products, see
[GRV, Proposition 3.6.11]).
Also, it is clear that a composition of mock-proper maps is mock-proper.
2.6.3. Examples.
• A proper schematic map between AG tame prestacks is mock-proper.
• For any algebraic group G, the stack pt /G is mock proper.
• Combining the previous two examples, we obtain that if Y is a proper scheme and G is an
algebraic group acting on Y , then Y/G is mock-proper.
• The stack An/Gm is mock-proper, where Gm acts on An by dilations.
• A cotruncative open embedding (see [DrGa2, Sect. 3]) is mock-proper.
2.6.4.
One can show that if a map f : Y1 →Y2 between schemes is mock-proper, them it is actually
proper.
Indeed, it follows from [AGKRRV2, Theorem B.6.3(a) and Proposition B.4.4(b)] that if f is mock-
proper, then f! →f∗is an isomorphism. However, the latter implies that f is proper.
2.6.5.
We will say that a map
f : Y1 →Y2
between prestacks is mock-smooth, if the corresponding 1-morphism
f ! : Shv(Y2) →Shv(Y1)
is adjointable (in AGCat).
Remarks pertaining to the notion of mock-properness in Sect. 2.6.2 are equally applicable to the
notion of mock-smoothness.
2.6.6. Examples.
• A smooth schematic map is mock-smooth.
• Let Y be a smooth quasi-compact algebraic stack. Then it is mock-smooth if and only if it is
safe7 (see [GaVa, Sect. 1.3(d)]).
• A truncative locally closed embedding (see [DrGa2, Sect. 3]) is mock-smooth.
• In particular, the map
pt /Gm →An/Gm
is mock-smooth.
2.6.7.
One can show that a map f : Y1 →Y2 between schemes is mock-smooth if and only if the
constant sheaf of Y1 is ULA with respect to f.
7“Safe” means that any constructible sheaf on Y is compact, see [DrGa1, Sect. 9].

2.6.8.
The following assertion results from the description of the unit and counit of the adjunction in
Sect. 2.5.6:
Proposition 2.6.9. Assume that:
(i) The action map G1 × (Y/G2) →(Y × Y)/G2 is mock-proper;
(ii) The stack Y/G1 is mock-proper;
(iii) The action map G2 × (Y/G1) →(Y × Y)/G1 is mock-smooth;
(iv) The stack Y/G2 is mock-smooth.
Then the 1-morphism (2.19) is 2-adjointable.
2.6.10.
Combining Propositions 2.6.9 and 1.8.2 with Corollary 2.5.8, we obtain:
Corollary 2.6.11. Let Y be a prestack equipped with an action of G1 × G2, satisfying the assumptions
of Proposition 2.6.9. Then the object of Arr(2 -AGCat) given by (2.19) is 2-dualizable.
2.6.12. A non-example. Let us take G1 = 1 and G2 = G, so we are dealing with a 1-morphism
1AGCat - mod →Shv(G) - mod
given by a prestack Y.
Assume that Y = Y is a smooth separated scheme and G is connected. We claim that the conditions
of Proposition 2.6.9 are never satisfied unless Y is a union of points and G is unipotent (which is not
an interesting case).
Indeed, condition (ii) implies that Y is proper (see Sect. 2.6.4). Condition (iii) implies that the map
G × Y →Y × Y
is ULA, which implies that G acts transitively on each connected component of Y ,
Hence, (ii) and (iii) together imply that each connected component of Y is of the form G/P, where
P ⊂G is a parabolic subgroup. Now, condition (iv) says that pt /P is mock-smooth, and hence safe.
This implies that P is unipotent, which in turn implies that P = G is unipotent.
2.6.13. The key example. Let us take G2 = G to be a connected reductive group, and G1 = M =
P/N(P) to be the Levi quotient of a parabolic P. Take
Y := G/N(P),
with the natural action of G × M.
We claim that this Y satisfies the conditions of Proposition 2.6.9.
Condition (i) holds because the map in question is schematic and proper. Indeed, the map
M × Y →Y × Y
is a closed embedding; this is an expression of the fact that M acts freely on Y with quotient G/P.
Condition (ii) holds because Y/M = G/P is proper.
Condition (iii) holds because the map in question is schematic and smooth. Indeed, the map
G × Y →Y × Y
is smooth because the action of G on Y is transitive.
Finally, condition (iv) holds because Y/G = pt /N(P) is smooth and safe, hence it is mock-smooth.
2.6.14.
Thus, by Corollary 2.6.11, we conclude that for Y
= G/N(P), the resulting object of
Arr(2 -AGCat) is 2-dualizable.
We will apply to this example the paradigm of Sect. 1.8.4 and in particular Corollary 1.8.7 in order
to obtain Deligne-Lusztig representations, and compute their traces.

In this section we will be concerned with calculating traces (in the ambient 3-category 2 -AGCat)

these traces).

3.3.6.
One can imagine wanting to compare the Grothendieck-Springer functors for different choices
of a parabolic with the same Levi quotient, using the above 2-morphism IP1,P2.
The problem is, however, that this 2-morphism is not adjointable, and so we cannot apply Sect. 1.5.1.
In particular, it does not induce a natural transformation
GrothSprP1 →GrothSprP2 .
And indeed, it is know that no such (non-zero) natural transformation exists: this is evidenced by
[Gu, Theorem E].
We will show how to remedy this by replacing the objects G - mod and M - mod by their relatives,
denoted G - modspec.fin and M - modspec.fin, respectively.

4. The condition of (quasi-)monodromicity
As was explained in the Introduction, in order to obtain a theory with interesting representation-
theoretic applications, we will need to replace the 2-category G - mod by its full subcategory
G - modspec.fin.
Its definition is based on the notion of monodromicity, which we develop in this
section.
Throughout this section, we will assume that G is connected.
4.1. The category of quasi-monodromic sheaves on a group.
4.1.1.
Let X be a connected scheme. Let
(4.1)
Shv(X)q-const ⊂Shv(X)
be the full subcategory, consisting of objects all of whose cohomologies (with respect to either the
perverse or the usual t-structure) are (possibly infinite) extensions of the (shifted) constant sheaf.
4.1.2.
We will be interested in the case when X = G is a group.
In Sect. A.1.10 we will prove:
Lemma 4.1.3. The category Shv(G)q-const is compactly generated.11
4.1.4.
We claim:
Lemma 4.1.5. For a pair of groups G1, G2, the functor
⊠: Shv(G1)q-const ⊗Shv(G2)q-const →Shv(G1 × G2)
is an equivalence onto
Shv(G1 × G2)q-const ⊂Shv(G1 × G2).
The lemma will be proved in Sect. A.3.
Remark 4.1.6. One can formulate statements parallel to Lemmas 4.1.3 and 4.1.5, when instead of
group(s) we have arbitrary (connected) scheme(s). However, we are not sure whether the corresponding
statements are true.
That said, they are true for simply-connected schemes; in fact our proofs apply in this case.
4.1.7.
Since the !-pullback functor (along a smooth morphism) maps the q-constant categories to one
another, from Lemma 4.1.5 we obtain:
Corollary 4.1.8. The functor
mult! : Shv(G) →Shv(G × G)
induces a comonoidal structure on Shv(G)q-const.
11Note, however, that the embedding (4.1) does not preserves compactness.

4.1.9.
Let χ be a multiplicative 1-dimensional local system (a.k.a., abelian character sheaf) on G. Let
Shv(G)q-χ ⊂Shv(G)
be the full subcategory equal to the essential image of
(−) ⊗χ : Shv(G)q-const →Shv(G).
It follows from Corollary 4.1.8 that the functor mult! induces a comonoidal structure on Shv(G)q-χ.
4.1.10. Example. Here is a typical example of an object in Shv(G)q-χ - comod(DGCat): we take the
underlying object of DGCat to be Vect, with the coaction functor
Vect →Vect ⊗Shv(G)q-χ ≃Shv(G)q-χ
given by χ.
Denote this object by Vectχ.
Remark 4.1.11. It is not true that Vectχ generates Shv(G)q-χ - comod(DGCat), i.e., the functor
HomShv(G)q-χ - comod,DGCat(Vectχ, −) : Shv(G)q-χ - comod(DGCat) →DGCat
is not necessarily conservative.
A parallel fact is true once we replace Shv(G)q-χ by a closely related category Shv(G)alm-χ (see
Sect. 4.6.1).
4.1.12.
Set
Shv(G)q-mon := ⊕
χ Shv(G)q-χ,
where the direct sum is taken over the set of (isomorphism classes of) χ’s.
Note that the tautological functor
(4.2)
Shv(G)q-mon →Shv(G)
is fully faithful:
It is clear that χ1 ̸= χ2 ⇒HomShv(G)(χ1, χ2) = 0, and then we use the fact that Shv(G) is
left-complete in its t-structure, see [AGKRRV1, Theorem 1.1.6].
4.1.13.
By Corollary 4.1.8, we obtain that the functor mult! induces a comonoidal structure on
Shv(G)q-mon.
In addition, the
!⊗operation makes Shv(G)q-mon into a symmetric monoidal category. These two
structures combine to a commutative Hopf algebra structure on Shv(G)q-mon.
4.1.14.
Note that we have an equivalence in 2 -DGCat:
Shv(G)q-mon - comod(DGCat) ≃⊕
χ Shv(G)q-χ - comod(DGCat).

Let
Shv(G)mult-decomp ⊂Shv(G)
be the full subcategory, consisting of objects F ∈Shv(G), for which mult!(F) belongs to the full
subcategory
Shv(G) ⊗Shv(G) ⊂Shv(G × G).
By Corollary 4.1.8, we have
(4.3)
Shv(G)q-mon ⊂Shv(G)mult-decomp
In Sect. A.5 we will prove:
Theorem 4.1.16. The inclusion (4.3) is an equivalence.

4.3. Proof of Theorem 4.2.7.
4.3.1. Proof of Theorem 4.2.7(a). To prove point (a), it suffices to show that for any C ∈AGCat, the
functor
C(X) ⊗Shv(G)q-mon →C(X × G)
is fully faithful.
We factor the above functor as
(4.6) C(X)⊗Shv(G)q-mon →C(X)⊗Shv(G) ≃(C⊗i(Shv(G)))(X) →(C⊗Shv(G))(X) ≃C(X×G),
and we note that the third arrow is fully faithful for any C (since i(Shv(G)) →Shv(G) is fully faithful
and admits a right adjoint).
Hence, the required assertion follows from the next proposition, which will be proved in Sect. A.4:
Proposition 4.3.2. The functor
C ⊗Shv(G)q-mon →C ⊗Shv(G)
is fully faithful for any C ∈DGCat.
4.3.3. Proof of Theorem 4.2.7(b). It suffices to show that if C is equipped with a map (2.9) that makes
(2.10) commute, then the essential image of (2.9) lies in
C ⊗Shv(G)q-mon ⊂C ⊗Shv(G),
provided that C is dualizable.
I.e., it suffices to show that the functor
C ⊗(Shv(G)/ Shv(G)q-mon) →C ⊗(Shv(G × G)/(Shv(G) ⊗Shv(G))) ,
induced by mult!, is conservative.
The functor
Shv(G)/ Shv(G)q-mon →Shv(G × G)/(Shv(G) ⊗Shv(G))
is conservative by Theorem 4.1.16.
Now, for any conservative functor D1 →D2, the functor
C ⊗D1 →C ⊗D2
stays conservative if C is dualizable: indeed, we can rewrite C ⊗D as
HomDGCat(C∨, D).
□
4.4. The maximal quasi-monodromic subcategory. In this subsection we will show that given
an object C ∈G - mod, there is a well-defined operation that extracts its maximal quasi-monodromic
subobject.
4.4.1.
Consider again the functor embq-mon of (4.5). We claim that it admits a right adjoint, to be
denoted
(4.7)
C 7→Cq-mon.
Namely, the category Cq-mon(X) is the full subcategory of C(X) that consists of objects c for which
coactX(c) ∈C(X × G)
belongs to the full subcategory
C(X) ⊗Shv(G)q-mon ⊂C(X × G)
(see (4.6) above).
The tautological embedding
embq-mon : Cq-mon →C

4.4.2.
Let us describe the functor (4.7) in the geometric situation.
For a prestack Y equipped with an action of G, denote by
(4.8)
Shv(Y)q-mon ⊂Shv(Y)
the full subcategory consisting of objects F, for which
act!(F),
act : G × Y →Y
lies in the full subcategory
Shv(G)q-mon ⊗Shv(Y) ⊂Shv(G) ⊗Shv(Y) ⊂Shv(G × Y).
It is clear that (for any Y), the object Shv(Y)q-mon ∈DGCat naturally upgrades to an object of
Shv(G)q-mon - comod(DGCat).
4.4.3.
For Y as above, consider Shv(Y) ∈G - mod. Consider the corresponding object
Shv(Y)q-mon ∈G - mod,
see (4.7).
It is clear that for X ∈Sch,
Shv(Y)q-mon(X) = Shv(Y × X)q-mon
as subcategories of
Shv(Y)(X) = Shv(Y × X).
4.4.4.
We will now describe the sub-object
Shv(Y)q-mon ⊂Shv(Y)
in a particular situation.
Proposition 4.4.5. Suppose that Y is algebraic stack on which G acts with finitely many orbits. Then
the natural map
i(Shv(Y)q-mon) →Shv(Y)q-mon
is an isomorphism. In particular, the functor
Shv(Y)q-mon ⊗Shv(X) →Shv(Y × X)q-mon,
X ∈Sch
is an equivalence.
4.4.6.
Note that as a particular case (for G = 1) we obtain:
Corollary 4.4.7. Let Y be an algebraic stack with finitely many isomorphism classes of points. Then
the object
Shv(Y) ∈AGCat
is restricted, i.e., the functor
Shv(Y) ⊗Shv(X) →Shv(Y × X),
X ∈Sch
is an equivalence.
4.4.8.
As another particular case, for Y = G equipped with the left action on itself, we obtain:
Corollary 4.4.9. The object Shv(G)q-mon ∈AGCat
Shv(G)q-mon(X) := Shv(X × G)q-mon
is restricted, i.e., the functor
Shv(G)q-mon ⊗Shv(X) →Shv(G × X)q-mon,
X ∈Sch
is an equivalence.

4.6. Quasi-monodromic vs monodromic. In this subsection we introduce a variant of the “quasi-
constant/quasi-monodromic” condition, which exhibits better functorial properties.
We note, however, that when G = T is a torus (which is the case of primary interest), the two
conditions are the same.
4.6.1.

We have an inclusion

but it is not an equality as long as G has a non-trivial semi-simple part, see Sect. A.1.10.
4.6.2.
We have the following counterpart of Lemma 4.1.5:
Lemma 4.6.3. For a pair of groups G1, G2, the functor

is an equivalence onto
Shv(G1 × G2)alm-const ⊂Shv(G1 × G2).
Proof. The functor in question is fully faithful, preserves compacts, and sends the compact generator
of the left-hand side to the compact generator of the right-hand side.
□

and under the self-duality and the Verdier self-duality of Shv(G × X), the right adjoint of embmon

4.9.4.
Note that the subcategories
Shv(T)alm-const ,→Shv(T)q-const ⊂Shv(T)
are compatible with the (perverse) t-structure on Shv(T).
Moreover, it is easy to see that the embedding (4.32) induces an equivalence on the corresponding
bounded subcategories. Hence, it induces an equivalence on the bounded below categories (as both
categories are right-complete in their t-structures).
Recall also (see [AGKRRV1, Theorem 1.1.6]) that Shv(T) is left-complete in its t-structure. This
implies that Shv(T)q-const is also left-complete in its t-structure. Hence, the embedding (4.31) identifies
Shv(T)q-const with the left completion of Shv(T)alm-const.
Therefore, the assertion of the proposition is equivalent to the statement that Shv(T)alm-const is
actually left-complete in its t-structure.
4.9.5.
The category Shv(T)alm-const is compactly generated by the constant sheaf constT . Hence,
Shv(T)alm-const ≃C·(T)-mod.
We identify C·(T) ≃Sym(V [−1])-mod, where V is a finite-dimensional Qℓ-vector space (placed in

5. Spectrally finite and restricted categorical representations
In this section we will finally define the subcategory
G - modspec.fin ⊂G - mod,
and show that as an object of 2 -AGCat, it is tensored up from DGCat.
We will also show that the induction functor
indP : M - mod →G - mod
has an additional adjointability property when restricted to G - modspec.fin.
In this section we let G be a connected reductive group.

6. Applications to character sheaves
In this subsection we will recast the theory of character sheaves from the point of view of
G - modspec.fin by taking the trace.
We will encounter some (non-obvious) features of character sheaves (e.g., the functional equation
for the Grothendieck-Springer functor, and the ambidexterity of the (GrothSprP , rP )-adjunction) that
follow from the 3-categorical formalism.
6.1. Character sheaves–a reminder.
6.1.1.
For a scheme Y , consider the pair of categories
(6.1)
Shv(Y × (G/ Ad(G))) and Shv(Y × (N\G/N)/ Ad(T)),
where Ad(T) refers to the diagonal action of T on N\G/N.
We regard the two sides of (6.1) as monoidal categories with respect to convolution.


## Page 92


92
D. GAITSGORY, N. ROZENBLYUM AND Y. VARSHAVSKY
6.1.2.
We have a monoidal functor
HC : Shv(Y × (G/ Ad(G))) →Shv(Y × (N\G/N)/ Ad(T)),
given by pull-push along the diagram
(6.2)
Y × G/ Ad(B) −−−−−→Y × (N\G/N)/ Ad(T)
y
Y × G/ Ad(G).
Since the horizontal morphism in (6.2) is smooth and the vertical one is proper, the functor HC
admits a left adjoint, to be denoted CH, which commutes with !-pullbacks and *-pushforwards along
Y .
The functor CH is left-lax monoidal, but is strictly compatible with the convolution action of the
monoidal category Shv(Y × (G/ Ad(G))) on either side.
In particular, we obtain an adjunction between the corresponding objects in AGCat:
CH : Shv(Y × (N\G/N)/ Ad(T)) ⇄Shv(Y × (G/ Ad(G))) : HC .
6.1.3.
We note:
Lemma 6.1.4. The endofunctor CH ◦HC of Shv(Y × (G/ Ad(G))) is isomorphic to the convolution
with the Springer sheaf.
Proof. This is obtained from Lemma 5.2.13 by passing to G-invariants on both sides.
□
6.1.5.
Since the Springer sheaf contains δ1,G as a direct summand, we obtain:
Corollary 6.1.6. The functor HC is conservative.
6.1.7.
Let
Shvch(Y × (G/ Ad(G))) ⊂Shv(Y × (G/ Ad(G)))
be the full subcategory consisting of objects whose image along HC lands in
Shv(Y × (N\G/N)/ Ad(T))mon ⊂Shv(Y × (N\G/N)/ Ad(T)).
Since HC is monoidal, from Corollary 5.3.6 we obtain that Shvch(Y × (G/ Ad(G))) is a monoidal ideal
inside Shv(Y × (G/ Ad(G))).
It is clear that for f : Y →Y ′, the *-pushforward and !-pullback functors along f × id map the
categories Shvch(Y × (G/ Ad(G))) and Shvch(Y ′ × (G/ Ad(G))) to one another.
6.1.8.
Thus, we obtain a well-defined object
Shvch(G/ Ad(G)) ∈AGCat,
equipped with a fully-faithful 1-morphism15
(6.3)
embch : Shvch(G/ Ad(G)) →Shv(G/ Ad(G)).
15The notion of fully faithfulness for a 1-morphism is intrinsic to any 2-category: it means that the induced functor
on Maps(−, −) is fully faithful. In the case of AGCat, this is equivalent to the fact that the given 1-morphism evaluates
to a fully faithful functor in DGCat on every scheme.

7. Further properties of the Grothendieck-Springer functor
The main goal of this section is to establish the transitivity property of the functional equation (this
is Theorem 7.1.2 below).
In addition, we will use the Serre functor on indP to give streamlined proofs for assertions about
the Harish-Chandra transform of character sheaves.
7.1. Transitivity of the functional equation.
7.1.1.
Let P1, P2 and P3 be three parabolic subgroups of G containing a common Levi subgroup M.
Consider the natural transformations

We will prove the following assertion:
Theorem 7.1.2. There exists an somorphism
(7.1)
iP2,P3 ◦iP1,P2 ≃iP1,P3
as natural transformations GrothSprch

P3.
The next few subsections are devoted to the proof of this theorem.
Remark 7.1.3. Our construction of the isomorphism (7.1) depends on some combinatorial choices.
However, we conjecture that it is actually canonical, i.e., we have a Grothendieck-Springer functor
GrothSprch
M,G : Shvch(M/ Ad(M)) →Shvch(G/ Ad(G))
that only depends on the pair M and G, and moreover it does so in a canonical way.
In particular, we conjecture that the functor GrothSprch
M,G is acted on by Norm(M)/M.
Note that for M = T, this means that the corresponding functor GrothSprch
T,G is acted on by the
Weyl group. (Note that this is not in contradiction with [Gu, Theorem D], as the latter talks about
all of Shv(M/ Ad(M)) and not GrothSprch
M,G.

7.1.5.

7.2. The implication Theorem 7.1.6 ⇒Theorem 7.1.2.
7.2.1.
Let P ′ and P ′′ be a pair parabolics sharing a Levi subgroup.
We shall say that they are
adjacent if there exists a parabolic Q containing P ′ and P ′′, and such that the projections of P ′ and
P ′′ to L := Q/N(Q) are sub-maximal opposite parabolics in L.
7.2.2.
We have the following combinatorial statement17:
Lemma 7.2.3. For a pair of parabolics P1 ̸= P2 sharing a Levi subgroup, let P ′
1 be another such
parabolic adjacent to P1. Then:
(a) Exactly one of the following two scenarios occurs:
(1) Either the relative positions P1, P ′
1, P2 add up;
(2) Or the relative positions P ′
1, P1, P2 add up.
(b) There exists P ′
1 so that scenario (1) occurs.
Proof. We will prove point (a); point (b) is proved along the same lines.
Recall that for a pair of parabolic subgroups Q1 and Q2 (with Levi quotients L1 and L2, respectively),
the image of the projection
Q1 ∩Q2/Q1 ∩N(Q2) →M2
is a parabolic subgroup in M2, and similarly for 1 and 2 swapped.
For P1 and P ′
1 as in the lemma, let Q be a parabolic that contains P1 and P ′
1 submaximally; let L
denote its Levi quotient. Denote by Q1 and Q′
1 the images of
P1 ∩Q →L ←P ′
1 ∩Q,
respectively. They are two submaximal opposite parabolics with Q1 ∩Q′
1 ≃M.
Let Q2 denote the image of Q ∩P2 in L. Since M ⊂P2, we obtain that Q2 contains (the image of)
M. Thus, we obtain that Q2 is yet another submaximal parabolic in L that contains (the image of)
M. This implies that either Q2 = Q1 or Q2 = Q′
1.
By symmetry, let us assume the latter. We claim that in this case the relative positions P1, P ′
1, P2
add up, i.e.,
N(P1) ∩N(P2) ⊂N(P ′
1).
Choose a maximal torus T ⊂M, so that we can consider the root decomposition with respect to
it. Thus, let α be a root in N(P1) ∩N(P2). We claim that α is in fact contained in N(Q); this would
imply that α is contained in N(P ′
1) as N(Q) ⊂N(P ′
1).
Indeed, if α /∈N(Q), then α ∈N(Q1). Since α ∈N(P2), we also have α ∈N(Q2). However, since
Q1 and Q2 are opposite parabolics in L, there are no roots common to N(Q1) and N(Q2), which is a
contradiction.
□
7.2.4.
Let us show how Lemma 7.2.3 and Theorem 7.1.6 imply Theorem 7.1.2.
We argue by induction on the distance P1 and P2, i.e., dim(P1/P1 ∩P2) = dim(P2/P1 ∩P2).
The base case is when P1 = P2, in which case there is nothing to prove.

8. Applications to the Deligne-Lusztig theory
In this section we will apply the theory developed in the previous sections and derive (both old and
new) results about the Deligne-Lusztig functor.
8.1. Trace of Frobenius on restricted representations. In this subsection, we show that (unlike
the case of the identity functor), if we take the trace of Frobenius on G - modspec.fin (resp., G - modrestr),
we recover all of Rep(G(Fq)) (resp., Rep(G(Fq))).
8.1.1.
Consider again the 1-morphism
embspec.fin : G - modspec.fin →G - mod.
It is adjointable and compatible with the Frobenius automorphism on the two sides.
Hence, it
induces a 1-morphism
(8.1)
Tr(Frob, G - modspec.fin) →Tr(Frob, G - mod) ≃Rep(G(Fq)).
in AGCat.
8.1.2.
We claim:
Theorem 8.1.3. The 1-morphism (8.1) is an equivalence.
Remark 8.1.4. Theorem 8.1.3 is due to A. Eteve. The proof that we give is a reformulation of the
argument in [Ete, Theorem 1.3.1].
8.1.5.
As an immediate corollary of Theorems 8.1.3 and 5.1.10(b), we obtain:
Corollary 8.1.6. There exists a canonical equivalence
Tr(Frob, G - modrestr) ≃Rep(G(Fq)),
whose image under i produces the equivalence of (8.1).
The rest of this subsection is devoted to the proof of Theorem 8.1.3.
8.1.7.
The fact that (8.1) is fully faithful follows immediately from Corollary 1.7.10. Hence, it remains
to identify its essential image.
8.1.8.
We have to show that the essential image of

is constructed by (8.17) rather than by the procedure in Sect. 8.6.3.

Appendix A. Sheaves on connected groups
In this section we let G be connected. We will supply proofs for some technical results from Sect. 4.
A.1. The categories Shv(G)q-const and Shv(G)alm-const. In this subsection we will provide an explicit
description of the categories Shv(G)q-const and Shv(G)alm-const.
A.1.1.
Note that the categories Shv(G)q-const and Shv(G)alm-const do not change when we replace G
by its maximal reductive quotient.
Hence, for the rest of this subsection we will assume that G is reductive.
A.1.2.
Let p : eG →G be an isogeny. It is again easy to see that the functor
p! : Shv(G) →Shv( eG)
induces equivalences
Shv(G)q-const ≃Shv( eG)q-const and Shv(G)alm-const ≃Shv( eG)alm-const.
Hence, we can assume that G is a product of a torus and a semi-simple group.
A.1.3.
We claim:
Lemma A.1.4. For a torus T and any (smooth, connected) scheme X, the functor
(A.1) Shv(T)alm-const ⊗Shv(X)q-const Proposition 4.9.2
≃
Shv(T)q-const ⊗Shv(X)q-const →Shv(T ×X)q-const
is an equivalence.
Proof. First we claim that (A.1) is fully faithful. This follows from the commutative diagram
Shv(T)alm-const ⊗Shv(X)q-const
(A.1)
−−−−−→Shv(T × X)q-const
y
y
Shv(T)alm-const ⊗Shv(X)
−−−−−→
Shv(T × X),
in which:
• The right vertical arrow is fully faithful tautologically;
• The left vertical arrow is fully faithful because Shv(T)alm-const dualizable;
• The bottom horizontal arrow is fully faithful, since it can be decomposed as
Shv(T)alm-const ⊗Shv(X) →Shv(T) ⊗Shv(X) →Shv(T × X)
with both arrows fully faithful (the first one since Shv(X) is dualizable).
Thus, it remains to show that the essential image of (A.1) generates the target. It suffices to show
that the pullback functor
π∗: Shv(X)q-const →Shv(T × X)q-const
generates the target.
It is easy to see that the functor
π∗: Shv(T × X) →Shv(X)
sends Shv(T × X)q-const →Shv(X)q-const.
Hence, it suffices to show that π∗is conservative on Shv(T ×X)q-const. Taking the !-fiber at some/any
point x ∈X, we reduce the assertion to one about T itself, in which case it follows from Proposi-
tion 4.9.2.
□

A.1.5.
Thus, using Lemma A.1.4, and given the description of
Shv(T)alm-const ≃Shv(T)q-const
in Sect. 4.9.5, we can focus on the case when G is semi-simple.
A.1.6.
First, as in Sect. 4.9.5, we have:
Shv(G)alm-const ≃C·(G)-mod,
where the functor ←sends
C·(G) ∈C·(G)-mod
to
ωG ∈Shv(G)alm-const.
Since G is semi-simple, we have
C·(G) ≃Sym(W),
where W is a finite-dimensional cohomologivally graded vector space concentrated in odd degrees ≥3.
The fiber of !-fiber at 1 ∈G identifies with the functor
(A.2)
(−)
⊗
Sym(W ) Qℓ,
Sym(W)-mod →Vect .
A.1.7.
Koszul duality identifies
Sym(W)-mod ≃Sym(W ∨[−1])-mod{0},
where the subscript {0} corresponds to the condition that the generators of W ∨[−1] act locally nilpo-
tently.
In terms of this equivalence, the functor (A.2) corresponds to the functor
Sym(W ∨[−1])-mod{0} →Sym(W ∨[−1])-mod →Vect,
where the second arrow is the natural forgetful functor.
In particular, the t-structure on Shv(G)alm-const corresponds to the t-structure on the category
Sym(W ∨[−1])-mod{0}, induced by the natural t-structure on Sym(W ∨[−1])-mod.
Remark A.1.8. We should think of the vector space W ∨[−1] as the abelian Lie algebra that governs
the rational homotopy type of G.
A.1.9.
Note now that as in Sect. 4.9.4, the category Shv(G)q-const identifies with the left-completion
of Shv(G)alm-const with respect to its t-structure.
From here, we obtain:
Shv(G)q-const ≃Sym(W ∨[−1])-mod.
A.1.10.
Note that the above description of Shv(G)q-const implies that it is compactly generated. In
particular, this implies Lemma 4.1.3.
Note, however, that the natural embedding
Shv(G)q-const →Shv(G)
does not preserve compactness. Indeed, it sends the compact generator
Sym(W ∨[−1]) ∈Sym(W ∨[−1])-mod
to an unbounded complex, denoted
q-constG ∈Shv(G)q-const,
whose fiber at 1 ∈G is Sym(W ∨[−1]).
It is also makes it manifest that the embedding
Shv(G)alm-const ⊂Shv(G)q-const
is not an equivalence.

A.5. Proof of Theorem 4.1.16. The proof presented below was explained to us by S. Raskin.
A.5.1.
Let F be an object of Shv(G)mult-decomp. We claim that in this case its perverse cohomologies
sheaves hi(F) also belong to Shv(G)mult-decomp.
Indeed, this follows from the fact that the functor mult! is t-exact up to a cohomological shift, and
the functor
Shv(G) ⊗Shv(G)
⊠
→Shv(G × G)
is t-exact, so its essential image is preserved by the cohomological truncations.
A.5.2.
Similarly, if F ∈Shv(G)mult-decomp is perverse, then its irreducible subquotients also belong to
Shv(G)mult-decomp.
Hence, we can assume that F is irreducible.
A.5.3.
The (cohomologically shifted) functor mult! sends irreducible perverse sheaves to irreducible
perverse sheaves. Irreducible objects of (Shv(G) ⊗Shv(G))♡have the form
F1 ⊠F2,
Hence,
(A.4)
mult!(F) ≃F1 ⊠F2.
Let U ⊂G (resp., U1, U2) be the maximal open subset over which F (resp., F1, F2) is lisse.
We obtain that
mult−1(U) = U1 × U2.
However, it is easy to see that this forces U = U1 = U2 = G. Hence, F (and hence F1 and F2) are
irreducible local systems.
A.5.4.
Restricting (A.4) to 1G × G, we obtain
F ≃(F1)1G ⊗F2.
Since F is irreducible, we obtain that F1 is one-dimensional, and F ≃F2.
Symmetrically, we obtain that F2 is one-dimensional, and F ≃F1.
A.5.5.
Thus, F is a one-dimensional local system, and we have an isomorphism
(A.5)
mult!(F) ≃F ⊠F.
I.e., F is an abelian character sheaf on G.
□[Theorem 4.1.16]
